What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I'm here because things have been a bit out of hand lately. You see, I'm the chief executive of my company, built it from the ground up, actually. Started with almost nothing, didn't even finish ninth grade, but I worked hard, you know? I pride myself on being a steady hand at the wheel. But just the other day, we had this board call. It was supposed to be a routine update, going over some basic figures, nothing we haven't done a hundred times before. And one of the junior members, he asked a perfectly reasonable, really quite small question about the facility budget for the next quarter. Just a standard inquiry about maintenance allocations or something trivial like that. And I... I don't know what came over me. I completely snapped at him. I mean, I raised my voice, I cut him off, I was incredibly harsh. It was entirely uncalled for, completely out of proportion to what he was asking. And the thing is, that is just not how I operate. It's not how I run my company, and it's certainly not how I treat the people who work for me. I believe in respect, in fostering a good environment, and this... this outburst, it really shook me. It made me realize that maybe I'm not managing things as well as I thought I was, because that level of irritability, that aggression over something so minor... it's just not me at all.\n\nDoctor: Has anything like this happened before—where you reacted in a way that felt out of character or lost control of your emotions in a similar situation?\n\nPatient: No, no, that's exactly why I'm sitting here talking to you today. If this was a regular occurrence, well... I wouldn't have much of a company left to run, would I? I've been in business for over forty years. I started with a single beat-up truck, hauling scrap metal because nobody was checking to see if I had a high school diploma, and you don't get from there to a corner office with a mahogany desk by flying off the handle every time someone asks a simple question. \n\nI mean, I've sat through negotiations that would make your hair curl. Union disputes back in the late nineties, supply chain disasters where millions of dollars were on the line, competitors actively trying to run me out of town... and through all of that, every single bit of it, I was always the guy who kept his voice down. I've always believed that the loudest guy in the room is the weakest guy in the room, you know? It shows you've lost your leverage. So I pride myself on being a rock. Even my late wife used to say I was almost too calm sometimes, like nothing could rattle my cage. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no. I have never just lost control of my emotions like that. Not at work, not at home, not anywhere. When I snapped at that young man during the board call... honestly, it was like watching somebody else do it. I was listening to the words coming out of my own mouth and thinking, 'Who is this guy?' Because I just don't have a history of this kind of behavior. It's completely out of the blue, completely foreign to how I operate, which is why it's been eating at me so much over the last few days. I just don't do things like that.\n\nDoctor: Since that board call, have you noticed yourself becoming unusually irritable or having difficulty controlling your emotions in other situations?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. And I can tell you that for a fact because I've been paying very, very close attention to it. Ever since that call, I've been... well, I suppose you could say I've been watching my own reactions like a hawk. I keep waiting for it to happen again, you know? To see if I'm losing my edge or my temper, but it just hasn't. \n\nFor example, just yesterday morning, my executive assistant, Sarah—she's been with me for fifteen years, wonderful woman, absolute salt of the earth, but she can be a bit scattered sometimes—she completely mixed up my morning schedule. Put a major vendor meeting right in the middle of a site inspection I was supposed to be doing across town. Now, a lot of guys in my position would have thrown a fit, especially if they were already feeling on edge. But I just... I didn't feel that hot flash of anger I felt on the board call. I didn't feel anything out of the ordinary at all. I just calmly asked her to fix it, just like I always do. I said, \"Sarah, let's reschedule the vendor,\" and that was that. \n\nAnd it's the exact same outside the office, too. I was driving home on Tuesday, and the traffic on the interstate was just backed up for miles, an absolute parking lot. Some fellow in a little red sedan cut me off so closely he nearly took off my front bumper. And you know, a younger man might have laid on the horn or yelled out the window, but I just tapped the brakes and let him go. Didn't bother me a bit. I haven't been snapping at the gal who gets my coffee wrong at the diner, I haven't been glaring at people in the elevator... nothing like that. \n\nIt really just seems to be that one isolated incident during the meeting. It's like a switch flipped in my head for about two minutes, and then it flipped right back off and I was back to being me. But the fact that it flipped at all, well, that's the puzzle, isn't it? I just can't figure out why it happened that one time and hasn't happened since. It makes no sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep or energy levels recently, such as trouble sleeping, sleeping too much, feeling unusually tired, or having bursts of energy?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Energy? No, no, nothing like that. I mean... I’m a creature of habit, Doc. Have been since I was pulling scrap metal in my twenties. You learn early on that if you don't get your rest, you make mistakes, and mistakes cost money. So, no, my sleep is exactly the same as it’s always been. \n\nI go to bed at 10:30 PM sharp. Not 10:45, not 10:15. 10:30. I read the trade papers or maybe a biography for exactly thirty minutes—well, usually thirty minutes, sometimes twenty if it's been a particularly long day on the floor—and then the lamp goes off. I sleep straight through until 5:00 AM. Every single day. Even on weekends. My late wife, God rest her soul, used to joke that I could sleep through a freight train driving through our bedroom. I bought one of those firm memory foam mattresses a couple of years back, thought it might mess up my lower back, but it’s been absolutely wonderful. I hit the pillow and I am out cold. Even with this whole meeting incident hanging over my head, it hasn't kept me up for a single minute. So no trouble sleeping at all, and certainly no sleeping in. \n\nAnd as for my energy... well, it’s just my normal, steady baseline. You can't run a company my size if you're dragging your feet by two in the afternoon, right? I don't even drink coffee after noon anymore. Just a cup of decaf or maybe some iced water with a slice of lemon if I'm feeling parched. I haven't felt unusually tired, and I certainly haven't had any weird bursts of energy. I’ve always been a high-energy guy, but it's a very controlled, steady kind of energy. Not like these young hotshots today who pound three energy drinks, bounce off the walls for an hour, and then completely crash during a 3 PM strategy meeting. I pace myself. Always have. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no. I wake up at five, do my thirty minutes on the stationary bike, eat my oatmeal with a handful of blueberries, and I'm ready to tackle the day. Regular as clockwork. No changes there whatsoever.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time when you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or unusually irritable, where you didn't feel like you needed as much sleep as usual or where you were more talkative or active than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I just touched on the sleep part a minute ago, Doc. I absolutely need my sleep. If I don't get my six and a half hours, I'm no good to anybody, so I definitely haven't had any stretches where I felt like I could just skip it and keep going. And as for being... what did you say, unusually confident or energetic? No. No, not at all. \n\nI mean, look, when you sit in the big chair at a company like mine, you have to have a certain baseline level of confidence, right? You can't second-guess yourself when you're signing off on a multi-million dollar equipment purchase. But my confidence is... well, it's earned. It's built on forty years of knowing the industry inside and out, knowing exactly what a ton of copper is going to do on the market next quarter. It's not some sudden, out-of-the-blue feeling that I'm invincible or something. \n\nI've actually seen guys like that in the business world. Back in the late eighties, there was this fellow who ran a rival hauling outfit across the state line. Every now and then he'd go into these phases where he was just talking a mile a minute, bouncing off the walls, buying up fleets of trucks he couldn't possibly afford, acting like he was the king of the world. We all figured he was on something, to be honest. But he ended up bankrupt in under three years. I never operated like that. I'm a slow and steady kind of guy. Always have been. \n\nAnd as for being more talkative... well, I know I'm probably talking your ear off right now, but I'm just trying to give you the full picture so we can figure this out. In my day-to-day life, at the office? I'm actually much more of a listener. You learn a whole lot more by keeping your mouth shut and letting the other guy show his hand first. So no, I haven't had any periods of acting like a hotshot or running on fumes. It's just been business as usual, which is why that one time I snapped at the board meeting is just... it's really sticking in my craw, you know?\n\nDoctor: In the past few weeks, have you felt down or hopeless, or lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, \"hopeless\" is a strong word, Doc. I’m not a hopeless kind of guy. You don't build a multi-million dollar operation from the ground up if you're the type to just throw your hands up and give in to despair. There's always a solution, always a way forward. That's just how business works, and that's how life works. \n\nBut... the part about losing interest, or not feeling pleasure? That... yeah. That’s actually been bothering me quite a bit lately. It’s hard to explain, but things that usually settle me down at the end of the day, they just feel completely flat right now. \n\nFor example, I've always been a big fan of chess. I have this beautiful, heavy marble set up in my study—bought it on a trip to Florence back in '06, I think it was. Usually, I love to sit down in my armchair and just work through a complicated chess problem. It focuses the mind, you know? Clears away the noise of the day. Or my hiking notes. I've kept detailed logs of every trail I've done in this state since the late nineties. Elevation, weather, boot wear, you name it. I usually get a real kick out of going over them, planning out the next route for when I have a free weekend. \n\nBut the last couple of weeks? I sit down at that chessboard, and it's just pieces of rock. I open the hiking journals, and it's just ink on paper. Even going out for a nice dinner—which is usually a real highlight for me—it's the exact same story. I went to O'Malley's on Friday, my favorite steakhouse downtown. Been going there for twenty years, they know my order before I even walk through the door. They brought out my usual bone-in ribeye, cooked perfectly, and... nothing. I was just chewing meat. \n\nIt’s like I know, intellectually, that I should be enjoying these things. My brain is telling me, \"This is the good part, this is where you relax and feel happy,\" but I just can't get there. The feeling itself is just... missing. It's like the color has been washed out of my evenings, and I'm just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest or pleasure in your usual activities been happening most days, and lasting throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think here... yeah. Yeah, it’s a constant thing. It’s not just when I sit down in the evening, it’s hanging over me the entire day, from the minute I wake up. And I can tell you exactly when it started, too. It’s been exactly six days. \n\nSix days ago, we had... well, there was a major incident. I was doing a walkthrough of our new processing facility over on the east side. I always do the final walkthroughs myself, you know? You just can't trust the general contractors to catch the small details or the safety tolerances, and like I said, I built this company from the ground up, so I like to see things with my own two eyes. So I’m out there, got my hard hat on, walking the floor with the site manager, going over the punch list line by line. \n\nAnd then... out of nowhere, there was this explosion. Just a massive, deafening boom that rattled my teeth and shook the concrete right under my boots. And then the fire broke out. It was just... well, it was absolute chaos, Doc. The alarms screaming, the heat hitting you in the face, the thick black smoke filling up the rafters. We got everyone out, thank God. Nobody was seriously hurt, just some minor stuff, but... man. \n\nEver since that afternoon, ever since I got back home and scrubbed the soot off my hands, that’s when this whole... this flat, gray feeling settled in. It hasn't lifted once in those six days. I wake up, and it’s there while I’m eating my oatmeal. It's there while I'm sitting at my desk reviewing contracts, and it's there when I try to look at my chess board at night. Six days straight of just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Since the explosion at the facility, have you felt like you’ve been emotionally overwhelmed or unable to cope with what happened, or have you felt detached from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Well, emotionally overwhelmed... I wouldn't use those words, exactly. Like I said, I'm a problem solver. I've navigated market crashes, supply chain collapses, you name it. When a crisis hits, I cope because I have to. The buck stops with me. \n\nBut that other word you used... detached. That... yeah, that actually hits the nail right on the head. It is the strangest thing, Doc, and it's been throwing me completely off my game. \n\nLet me give you an example of what I mean. Obviously, since the fire, I've been working a lot out of the house, taking a mountain of calls from home. I’ve got the insurance adjusters on one line, the fire marshal on the other, trying to keep the board of directors from going into a total panic... it's a lot of high-stakes conversations. So I'll be sitting right there in my study—the same study with that marble chess set I mentioned earlier, sitting in my leather chair that I've had for fifteen years—and suddenly, right in the middle of a call, things just get... weird. \n\nThe whole room just sort of pulls away from me. It looks distant, almost like I'm looking at my own office through the wrong end of a pair of binoculars. The walls, the bookshelves, it all just feels far away and unreal. And the craziest part is, it's not just the room. It's me, too. I'll be right in the middle of explaining a safety protocol or authorizing a cleanup crew, and I can hear my own voice, but it sounds completely strange to me. Hollow, maybe? Or echoed? \n\nIt’s almost like... well, it's like I'm just watching myself talk instead of actually being the one sitting there having the conversation. Like I'm a spectator in my own body, just hovering there and observing this guy who looks and sounds like me, but I'm not fully there. It's incredibly distracting, let me tell you. When you're trying to negotiate with a hard-nosed insurance guy, you need all your wits about you. You can't afford to be floating off in the ether. But I just can't seem to shake it when it happens. I've never experienced anything like it in my fifty-nine years on this earth, and quite frankly, I don't like it one bit.\n\nDoctor: Since the explosion at the facility, have you found yourself reliving the event through sudden, intrusive memories or flashbacks, even when you didn’t intend to think about it?\n\nPatient: Reliving it... yeah, you could say that. And it’s infuriating, Doc, to be perfectly honest with you. Like I was saying before, I'm trying to manage the fallout of this whole mess. I've got spreadsheets open all day, I'm looking at the damage estimates, the quarterly projections, trying to figure out how we reroute our entire supply chain while the east side facility is shut down. I've got to keep the ship steering straight, right? \n\nBut I'll be right in the middle of a highly sensitive call with one of our top vendors, or just sitting there at my desk looking at the numbers on my screen, trying to do the math on the repairs... and suddenly, it just hits me. The blast. The way the fire just erupted over by the loading area. We had these massive, heavy-duty steel roll-up doors by those loading docks, and the image of that fire just swallowing that whole section up, it just jumps back into my head out of absolutely nowhere. \n\nI don't want to think about it. I have zero intention of thinking about it. I *need* to be focusing on the margins and the recovery plan. But it just forces its way in anyway. It’s exactly like... well, it’s like a bad film playing in my head that I didn't ask to see, and I can't find the switch to turn the damn thing off. I'll just be sitting there with the phone to my ear, completely derailed for a second because I'm seeing that blast and the flames by the loading area all over again. It’s incredibly frustrating. When you're used to being in control of your own focus, your own mind, and then something like this just barges in... it really throws a wrench in the gears.\n\nDoctor: When you say there was an explosion at the facility, was it something that you felt intense fear, helplessness, or horror during or immediately after it happened?\n\nPatient: Fear? Helplessness? No, no, no. Listen, Doc, you don't get to where I am by freezing up when things go sideways. Helpless is... well, it's just not a word in my vocabulary. Never has been. I've been working since I was a kid, you know? I left school before high school even started to make my own way in the world, so I learned early on that if you just stand around waiting for someone else to save you, you're going to get run over. You have to be the one steering the ship. \n\nSo when that blast actually hit... I mean, sure, it was incredibly loud, it knocked the wind right out of me, and the concrete dust was instantly so thick you could chew it. But horror? Fear? No, I really didn't have time to be horrified. The second I got my footing back, my brain just went straight into, you know, execution mode. I looked over and grabbed the site manager—he was coughing, looking a little dazed, frankly—and I practically dragged him by the vest toward the nearest exit, barking at him to get on his radio and trigger the emergency shutoffs. I was pointing the crew toward the fire stairs, making sure nobody was pinned under the scaffolding near the loading bays... it was all logistics, really. Just rapid-fire problem-solving. You assess the variables, you take command, and you get the job done. \n\nSo, no, I wasn't cowering in a corner or feeling terrified. I was the one getting people out the door and counting heads in the parking lot. It’s only *now*, you know, six days later, with this weird floating feeling and that image of the fire constantly interrupting my work, that things feel out of my control. But in the actual moment of the incident? No, absolutely not helpless. I was entirely in charge of the situation.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, sure, it was incredibly loud, it knocked the wind right out of me, and the concrete dust was instantly so thick you could chew it. But horror? Fear? No, I really didn't have time to be horrified. The second I got my footing back, my brain just went straight into, you know, execution mode. I looked over and grabbed the site manager—he was coughing, looking a little dazed, frankly—and I practically dragged him by the vest toward the nearest exit, barking at him to get on his radio and trigger the emergency shutoffs. I was pointing the crew toward the fire stairs, making sure nobody was pinned under the scaffolding near the loading bays... it was all logistics, really. Just rapid-fire problem-solving. You assess the variables, you take command, and you get the job done. \n\nSo, no, I wasn't cowering in a corner or feeling terrified. I was the one getting people out the door and counting heads in the parking lot. It’s only *now*, you know, six days later, with this weird floating feeling and that image of the fire constantly interrupting my work, that things feel out of my control. But in the actual moment of the incident? No, absolutely not helpless. I was entirely in charge of the situation."
  }
]
